TO ADVERTISERS.
The amount of advertising offered to u?
yesterday was more than sufficient to fill
the four pages of THE TRIBUNE this
morning. When this fact became known
to ns we were compelled either to exclude
onc-llxlrd of the whole, or to put those not
already in type into less space than ordered
by the advertisers. We decided to make
no discriminations for or against any of
our patrons, but to give all an equal chance,
for there is no aristocracy in Chicago
now. Those advertisements that were al
ready sot up when we learned the impossi
bility ol printing the whole arc deployed
according to order. The remainder arc set
solid, as the only means of getting them in
at all

SATURDAY, OCTOBER 14, 1871.
Tuc Chicago Tsubcto has opened its offices at
o. 15 South Canal street, West Division, and the
?cr will hereafter be regularly issued from that
ace till further notice.
Our readers arc requested to notice that we
rge the same price'(br TflK TRIBUNE as be-
ire the fire. Single copies can bo obtained for
vc cents over our counler, at No. 15 South Canal
rcct, aud there is no occasion to pay the exorbl-
•ml price asked by newsboys on the street.
HE DISTRIBUTION OF SUPPLIES.
There is an immediate necessity
nr prompt and vigorous action,
onclung the management of the
listribution of relief. It is possible
hat there are' many persons who
ire receiving liberal portions of the
supplies intended for those leffc des-
Uutc and homeless by the fire, but
his is more or less incidental to any
yslem, and it is safer to give to the
mworthy than give hesitatingly t*
lose in actual need. But there is
mch a tiling as actual waste. It is
>3ssiblo to register every person
md family forced by the fire to
cave their old residences, with the
lames and present stopping-places
Df each family, and thus gradually
jring within some system the dis
sipation of the food and other sup
)lies. In addition to the generous
.lock of food and clothing sent
lilher, the liberal, noble-hearted
>cople throughout the .land have
<ent here large sums of money.
This money ought not to be touehed
save for the sacred object of pur.
?hasing food and clothing. We are
is yet upon the mere threshold of
ihe suffering and distress resulting
:rom this fire. In a few weeks we
shall have rain, and snow, and frost,
with all the severity of along winter
upon us. Fuel will be as in
dispensable as bread. Every dollar
that has been sent to us, and the ag
gregate duplicated, will not suffice
to purchase the absolute necessaries'
{life for these unfortunates. There
>hnuld be uo distribution .of that
money for any purpose so long-as
there is any other means left to
draw upon.
AVo have had in Chicago, during
the part four years, a Relief Society
which, for efficiency and noble work,
has not been equalled. Mr. O. C.
(iiiibs is, perhaps, the best man in
the city to have the general charge
m this entire business, and wo
therefore urge that the Mayor -and
city authorities, and all others, trans
fer to the Chicago Aid and Relief
Society the entire and exclusive
management and control of the dis
mhution of every kind of public
aid. All money sent hither from
abroad should be left in the hands
of Mr. David A. Gage until needed
and called for by the society.
One reason why the Chicago Aid
and Relief Society have been able
to prevent waste, and to make each
dollar do as ranch good as ten dol
lars in the hands of others, is the
thorough system which it adopts,
and the aid it affords in the way of
enabling men to find employment,
and become self-supporting. After
all, the greatest aid that can be ex
tended, is to furnish employment.
Knelt man put at work will pay his
own way, and at much less cost
than will be incurred by supporting
him out of the public treasury.
Kacb man and woman entitled to
support, or aid, or relief, ought to
he recorded, and should be relieved
according to actual wants, of which
the agency should be personally in
formed. Indiscriminate distribution
is waste, and waste at this time, and
in view of the approaching season,
is criminal. Every pound of flour,
and of meat, every blanket and pair
of shoes will be needed for the really
.destitute; and every man put at
work, and thereby made self-sup
porting, leaves so much more for
distribution among the more unfor-
lunate.
The committees who have oorae
uere from other places withiSUßpUes
.mblisli an address to the public,
which is to the point They are
‘onscious that the present wont of
'Vsicm needs correction, -and they
nake timely suggestions that some
inited action r-hould take place to
-ecurc efficiency to render it certain
hat the supplies should really be
'evoted to the purpose for which
hey were designed. These supplies
.T-m intended for the sufferers by
he fire,and none others ;aud it is the
inly of our people to sec that every
■erson who has been rendered home*
i«s and destitute by the fire should
ml ns great relief as is possible,
nd to do this with any certainty re*
.uires that tbe distribution should
./put under charge of one general
rganizntion, and be conducted with
me degree of regularity and or-
CITT ELECTION'.
There seems to be some misap
rchension in the minds of many
oncerr.ing tho law of residence
ir voting purposes. Though the
oetion, in consequence of our re
nt calamity, will be but slightly
Unresting, it is desirable that what
ilonc should he done legally.
lie enforced absence of a voter
oes not destroy the legal residence;
yijencfe is a matter of intention,
id every man trill be a voter in the
ard and precinct where. he resided
ifore the firc,unless he has resolved
change his residence. Voters must
sercforc register and vote in their
friier precincts, even though there
;no actual resident in them. Al-
•mien and Supervisors must he
ccted in their respective wards by
e 'cgal voters thereof.
cm and conn v buildings. 1
The Board of Snpervisorshave,
an unanimous vote, ordered that
new Court House and offices be
ccted upon the site of the old one,
mg so much of the old building
<1 materials as may be available.
Ie Common Council have' also or
red that the city provide a new
ty Hall and offices—dither by re
aring tbf^U^MMHgcting'a’
resolved to erect tcmporaiy public
i buildings on the Quincy street reser
voir lot, thus leaving tbc public
square free for the operations of the
workmen.
Four hundred workmen can thus
be given employment immediately,
and that is the surest way to afford
relief to the destitute. At the pres
ent time there is no charity equal to
that which gives employment, and
makes men self-supporting.
WHAT THE LEGISLATUitE
CAN BO FOSt CHICAGO.
The State Legislature has assembled nf
Springfield, for the purpose of taking into
consideration the measures proper to be
adopted for the relief of Chicago in her
present emergency. It is due to the dig
nity of Chicago, ns well as to the welfare
of the State, that we should say, while
I earnestly requesting of the Legislature
sncli relief as they may sec fit to bestow,
that we do not ask that body to do for us
anything that is unconstitutional, or any-
I thing that is unreasonable, or anything
that is incompatible with their duties
to their constituents; that we do not
ask them to establish any precedents
which will be dangerous to the future
welfare of the Slate. We merely ask them
totdo what tbey cm toward rolling off
the great stone which crushes us down i
toward removing the debris of bricks and
stone and mortar under which we are this
moment buried.
As one measure of relief, It is oracti
cable, doubtless, for the State to assume
the maintenance of some of the charitable
and corrective institutions for which the
city or county is now held responsible.
The boys in the Reform School, for in
stance, might be removed to the State
institution at Pontine, where they could
be taken care of more economically than
here. There are several hundred insme
persons on our hands, of which the State
might relieve the county, us is already
done for several other counties.
Again, we ask the Legislature to remit
the State taxes next due from this city
and county, or at least to postpone their col
lection, and not send the Sheriff with hi-«
writs to rnkc among the ashed of the prop
erty on which those taxes were assessed
We ask it to relieve us of the canal debt of
$5,000,000, which we had assumed in
order to insure the completion of a State
work of great national importance. This
won:d enable us to rebuild the public edi
fices of the county and city, without ex
ceeding the constitutional limits of indebt
edness, which is now reached. In return,
we would, of course, abandon to the State
the lien which we now hold upon the reve
nues of the canal; the State could lake
complete possession or the work, and could
defray by taxation, not by borrowing,
the amount of the annual interest and the
bonus when they become due.
Finally, we psk the Legislature to dp
what it can constitutionally and legiti
mately to help maimed and crushed Chicago
on her feet again This will doubtless be
1 done heartily and generously though not
Imorogcncroualy than justly, since what
I ever helps Chicago to regain her facilities j
1 for making exchanges and forwarding to
the' consumers the vast food surplus of
Illinois helps the State at large. If any of
the members of the General Assembly sus
pect that the stories of our calamity have
been exaggerated, we beg of them to make
a journey to Chicago, and slay with us
over Sunday. They can doubtless do this
now without fears ot being entrapped into
Chicago session, and they will lie enabled
to comprehend more keenly than they
otherwise could the full extent of our
awful calamity and misery.
THE GREAT CENTRAL D£
POT.
The position cli THE TRIBUNE
has always taken in regard to the three
blocks of ground cast of Michigan avenue
and north of Monroe street, so long in con
troversy, has been that when llic three
railway companies there located wonld
pay the city what it was worth, they ought
to bo allowed to take it for the erection of
a great passenger depot. Wc understand
that these companies still have faith enough
in Chicago to pay eight hundred thousand
dollars for the ground, and expend three
millions of dollars upon it in the erection
of the finest depot in the whole country.
We ask, in all soberness, whether it is
not wise for the city to accept this offer.
Eight hundred thousand dollars is all that
the land is worth to-day. Eight hundred
thousand dollars would rebuild every edi
fice on the Court House Square, and the
city cannot obtain the money in any othc.
way, because its borrowing power has been
exhausted, and it dare not divert the money
in the water fund to any other purpose,
even supposing that fund to be available
Eight hundred thousand dollars would
set an army of men at work, and take
them and their families off the relief fund.
The locality mentioued has already been
surrendered to business, and the owners
die residence property fronting it have
commenced erecting stores there. Their
opposition to. the depot has been with
drawn. We hope the Common Oounc'l
will now withdraw theirs. The project of
the railroads docs not touch anything
south of Monroe street
Titc land in question has been tempora
rily leased, wc Ixdlevc, to private parties,
whose rights will, of cour/c, be respected
but tire wooden structures which they are
erecting will constitute no real obstacle to
the building of the great central depot on
a greater scale than ever before. For the
city to hold lbi-« ground now for specula
tive purposes would be the most prepos:
terous folly ever heard of. except that of
putting a wooden roof on the pumping
works.

THE GETTYSBURG!! PICTURE.
Don Pedro Carlo Stockel. the janitor of the
Academy of Design, has called upon ns with refer
enceto the saving of Rothermcrs great historical
painting of the "Battle of Oettysburgh.’’ "Don,"’
wlio slept in the Academy, was up all night. At C
o'clock he saw the danger to the Academy, and
at once determined to rescue the picture. The
drapery was tom away, and the mouldings remov
ed. and an effort was then made to wrench It from
the nails. In so doing the picture was torn some,
but not so badly that it cannot bn repaired. A*
Inst the picture was taken down and cm
from the stretcher, and “Don" carried it
to Jackson street and placed it on
the steps of Trinity Church. He then returned to
save lus personal effects and more pictures, and
when he went back come other parties had re
moved It to a place of safety, Ue remained In the
Academy, doing bis duty nobly, until the fire fairly
scorched him and drove him out. lie did his duty
nobly, and as he U going to New York wo com
mend tdm to the artists there as an honest, faithful
mao.
ORDER MAINTAINED.
Lieutenant General Sheridan yesterday addressed
the following commnnicatlon to the Mayor, giving
the gratifying intelligence that peace and good or
der prevail, bat calling upon the citizens to con
tinue for a time their vigilance and precautionary
measures.
Headquarters Military Division of tbc Missouri,
Chicago, Oct. I*2.—To Ills Honor the Mayor—The
preservation of peace and g*K)d order of the city
having been entrusted tome by Your Honor, I am
happy to state that no case of out
break or disorder has been reported. No
authenticated attempt at Incendiarism has reached
me. and that the people of the city are calm, quiet,
and well disposed.
The force at my disposal is ample to maintain
order should it bo necessary, and protect the dis
trict devastated by Arc. Still, 1 would suggest to
citizens not to relax in their watchfulness until
tho smouldering Arcs of the burnt buildings are
entirely extinguished. P. 11. SHERIDAN.
Lieutenant General.

BANKRUPTCY RECORDS.
Mr. H. N. Hubbard, the Register in Bankrnptcy
of Hie United States Court, has saved the entire
official records of his office. His clerk was there
st the time of the fire, and carried them out to the
prairie on the West side, where they now lie safe.
These records ore the more valuable, since the
originals, of which they are copies, wero burned
In the United Slates court room. Theyarcjhe
only official court records ns yet known to'bc saved

Church, corner of Washington and Green streets,

Church, at the corner of Sangamon and Adams,
where abont 1.000 people were ‘furnished, not only
with food, but with almost sumptuous food, three
times yesterday. Considerable clothing was also
distributed at the same place. The supplies were
chiefly obtained from tbo Relief Committee, but U
was through the ashldnons efforts of the ladies of
the Tenth Ward, assisted by a few gentlemen, that
the meals were served so promptly and bountifully,
and supplied with many dainties to add to the
relish of the banquet. The programme
will he repeated to-day and until further notice.
The geests were furnished with scats and tables
In the basement of the cbnrch. which makes a
spacious dining room, and probably never served
God better than now, while serving as tbo dis
pensary of food to the needy. The scene presented
by the long rows of sufferers was affecting in the
extreme. Manv showed very plainly the signs of
their recent suffering, gome were bandaged or de
faced, In token of physical injuries received dar
ing the conflagration; many were evidently of the
poorer class; hut not a few were apparently of a
class which had not only never before been
recipients of anything which resem
bled charity. hat bad never
dreamed of each a vicissitude coming upon them;
men who have shone In social and artistic cir
cles as both gay and gifted, came for their rations,
with their coats buttoned to conceal that memento
of the fire, almost universally worn now by Chicago
gentlemen—-a soiled shirt-front. Many of those
who are non* (and rightfully) receiving food and
lodging at the bands of those who have undertaken
these h»noflccni measures, were last week among
the most luxurious livers and enjoying the most
promising prospects of any In the city. Too much
credit cannot be given to the ladies who have vol
unteered their services and arc laboring so arduous
ly iu the relief canse.

HA MI LTO>*., C ANA D A
A committee from Hamilton. Canada, composed
of Messrs. Stanford, Wood, nnd Copp, arri cd In
Chicago on yesterday morning, and at once waited
upon Mayor Mason and C. C. P. Holden, with a
view to ascertaining the precise needs ol the sot
ferers. The Hamilton gentlemen have adopted a
course especially judicious and Intelligent under
the circumstances. Aware that prices In Canada
of everything needed are from 25 toTsper cent
lower than To the States, the committee wisely
fore bo re to forward anr money, preferring to in*
vest the contribution? in Canada, and thu* give
Chicago the foil benefit of the low prices there.
They were advised by Mayor Mason and President
Holden that the articles most needed now and in
the immediate funro are clothing, boots and
•hoc*, blankets, coal, hardware, nails, etc. With
this Information, wnich the people of Canada in
general will bo glad to act upon, the Hamilton
committee will at once return or send word borne,
and noble responses may bo expected at once.
They wore provided with a special car by the Great
Western I ailroad Company, in which they rode
through to Chicago and (n which thiy are living.

LETTER FROM S 9. HAYES.
Toi the EdltorofThe Chicago Tribune.
As the General Assembly -are about to meet,
under the call of tho Governor to legislate for the
relief of Chicago, suffering under the terrible ca
lamity which has befallen ns. I venture to make a
few suggestions,which may cull forth others, and aid
In producing a well defined and judicious plan. I
mention the following points:
To authorize a revaluation of property In the
city for Slate and connty taxes of IcCl—to relieve
the burnt district and the immediate sufferers as
Car a.-i possible.
2. To authorize a suspension of city taxes, put
for two years, and a temporary loan by the State to
defray the general expenses of the city, to be re
paid trom collections of taxes in future.
3. To memorialize Congress in general for such
assistance as they can constitutionally grant, and
In particular, as n part of tho relief, for an appropri
ation of four millions, to refund to Chicago the
amount expended in enlarging the Illinois and
Michigan Canal, and to pass such laws as to convey
ibe title of the canal and Us revenues to the Gen
eral Government. The General Government can
receive the canal anj complete it as a ship canal
tinder the power to regulate commerce between
the States and with foreign nations, and to provide
for the national defence.
•1. To authorize Chicago to make all necessary
arrangements with Its creditors for payment of the
city debt, principal and Interest, and old them in
doing so, as far as the power may exist.
1 w»»ild suggest also that the’Common Council
should invito the of a committee of oar
most enlightened and experienced citizens, in ar
ranging a financial scheme to meet the exigency.
Voure truly, s s u.WKi
Chicago, Oct. la, 1671.

To the Citizens of Chicago.
The undersigned respectfully call your attention
to tbc following facts The committees from the
principal cities of the West with food and supplies
of ail kinds have been in your city since last Mon
day night; they assembled at tho headquarters of
the Mayor and City Council, corner of Ann and
Washington streets, and have since ro-operatod
with Alderman Holden, and other members of the
Council. Mr. Moord and his associates being the
only organization known to them In the city for tho
relief of the sufferers by tbo great fire, the St.
Louis supplier l with largo quantities entrusted to
tbo delegation from Indiana and Illinois*, were dis
tributed by General Hardio, who in
under orders of General Sheridan, placed them,
as wo believe, most Judiciously. We attest most
heartily to the tm.-elCslj and arduous services
rendered by Alderman Holden, Mr. Mooro and his
associate incmls rs, the Mayor, and many of tho
Common Connell, Mr. Preston, of tho Board of
Trade, and especially General Sheridan and his
aids, and yet deem it a duty to say to you that It
is now absolutely -sscntlal that tbo work be sys
tematically and economically extended, that
ample arrangements fhnvhl at onet b°
made for the reception and careful dis
tribution of coming supplies by an
organization which will satisfy yourselves, and en
courage your friends to continued action. We arc
perfectly satisfied to recommend to the country
that all moneys intended for your relief bo sent to
the City Treasurer, because we believe that they
will not only bo safe, but will bo expended in ac
cordance with the wish of tho contributors but
from the facts presented we trust you will sec the
actual necessity for the systematic arrangement al
luded to: and. now that your best men can
calmly survey tho condition without fear of the fu
ture, we again mostcarnestly beg that you will take
immediate steps for n thorough and permanent
organization, that will be entirely equal to the
great work before them.
HENRY P. BLOW.
Ch. of the Western Committee?.
A. T. GOSUORN.
Cb. of Cincinnati Com.
WM. M. MORRIS,

NORTHWESTERN FIRES.
WISCONSIN.
The latest reports from Pesbtlgo arc to the effect
that three hundred and twenty-five dead bodies
had been found and buried, up to Wednesday
night, and it was believed that one hundred more
would be fonnd when the river was dragged. Be
tween sixty and seventy dead bodies have been
brought into Oconto. At the Belgian settlement
In Door County, nine persons were lost. Tts
Governor has authorized the Mayor of Green Bay
to drew on the State Treasury Ibr not exceeding
$20,000 to relieve sufferers.
MICHIGAN.
All that part of the State north of a point forty
1 miles above Fort Huron, and cast of Saginaw Bay.
has been swept by fire. Numerous villages have
been entirely destroyed. Several persons are
known to have been destroyed, but eighteen or
twenty picked up by a vessel. Tbe loss In
the State has amounted to hundreds of thousands
of dollars. Bridgeport was saved only by a shower
of rain.
ILLINOIS.
Last Monday a fire broke oat In the southern
part of Urbana, In Champaign County, which de
stroy cd five or six residences andelgbtor nine bn£
iness bouses. The loss 1# not less than $125,000.

FINANCIAL AND COMMERCIAL.
Financial lutclllgeucr,
THE KEELIN-. AMONG THE BANKERS.
ve>«erday, was much more cheerful than on the
previous day, when the resolutions In regard 'O the
probable coarse of the bunks was adopted.
We have talked with not less than fifteen or
eighteen prominent bankers, and find a disposition
on the part of several of them to resume
business us usual, paying checks of their
depositors for nil reasonable sums within ihe
amount of the balances duo thorn. The
Merchants' National and several other* may possi
bly adopt this coun*o. They nflinn positively their
ability at piy every dollar of their deposit* wtlhln
sixty day*, and express their willingness losubmit
to the unquestionable right of theirdeposltnrs to
check om their entire balances, but earnestly
hope they will not adopt n policy that
would be fraught with such harm to nil. If
the hanks could receive from their defaulters some
assurances of their confidence and their disposltiou
to sustain the hauks.lt would go a great way to
ward re-establishing business.
One important question arises in regard to out
standing checks drawn on Saturday, the Tth. and
ihat would, in the ordinary course of business,
have gone through the clearings on Monday morn
ing had it not been for the fire. We have conversed
with a number of prudent bankers ou this subject,
who are of the opinion that these checks should
be paid in full.—ln other words, that they should go
through tho clearing house as though no fire had
occurred. These checks arc now hold by the banks
against each oilier, and ought to bo balanced off
against caph other. It womd take comparatively
little money to do this, and would got one obstacle
out of the way of the resumption ot business.
The comptroller of the Currency will be In Chi
cago on Monday or Tuesday, to confer with ihe
National Hanks In regard lo the re-establishment
of their business.

expect to begin paying depositors early next week.

or two. The bank will begin plying by Instal
ment assoonas the twenty dollar payment to suf
erers t» disposed of.

UOMMKIUIAL,
Fimur Evesino, Oct. 13. J
To day was the fir?l of real business in produce
circles since the great fire suspend -d everything.
There was not much done, purchases being prin
cipally of small Jot? and those mostly for home
use, but the produce movement was fairly re
sumed, and the receiver? ami shippers of grain are
at work once mure.
There was a larger attendance on 'Change to-dar
and a great deal of animation, but the discussion
generally turned much less on the great question
siwrrogSTTte rcd all over the door talking up i
or down the proposal to adjourn 1
to Burlington Ilall, la the South
Division, in the neighborhood of Thirteenth
street. For the movement it was argued that the
banka are gathering In that locality, and that It
win be impossible to do business so tlir awav from
them. Against u was raised the objection that
the great majority of the public men. the telegraph
and express companies, and others, have already
engaged locations on tho West Side, and that tha*t
locality is nearest to the elevators, and most acces
sible from the packing districts, to say nothing of
the newspapers. Of course the arguments, nro
and con, are dictated by the consideration that tho
presence of the business centre will cmsc a large
improvement in the value of projwrtv in the neigh
borhood, wherever it naj be; and there are
plenty of parties, anxious to grasp the advantage,
uV IhOD Sb I* u>»T be but a tcm|»orary one. j
The facta to the case are that the Hoard of Trade
has taken a lease of It? present location for the sum
Of $1,5001 per year, and have also agreed to move
pack to the former location as soon as the building
IS reconstructed, which will bo accomplished as
soon as possible. The reoccupancy will be defer
red not more than twelve months. Under these
circumstances the stake Is certalulv a small one
and the disagreement about the matter Is ua dl*-
crcdltablc as a fight at a funeral.
The rivalry* developed between tho two sections
only furnishes one of many Instances in which his
tory repeats Itself. Thlrtr-elght rears ago the dis
cussion between the West Side (Wolfs Point) and
the Sonrh side of the river was ever more bitter
than now: each wanted to grow at the expense of
the other.
Th.* question of location will probably bn settled
to-morrow, (Saturday) at a o'clock, at the same
meeting tailed to decide whether contracts to de
liver produce that were outstanding at the time of
the flroahall bo cancelled or held good. The qnes
tlon cannot be disposed of in any way that wilt not
workg.eat hardship to manv parties, and action
upon u should bo taken only* after the roost ma
ture and comprehensive consideration of the case,
as steeling the dlfierem Interests involved.

adopted by ihe Hoard of Directors of the Hoard of
Trade to-cUy. October 13:
